## Lateen

A lateen was a style of sail used on various ships across Faerûn and Zakhara. Some Zakharans mistakenly believed that this style of sail was unique to ships in the Land of Fate and not found anywhere else on Toril.In the book Golden Voyages, it is stated that lateens are "a style not used in the northern or eastern lands". These lands most likely refer to Faerûn and Kara-Tur respectively. However, instances of lateen sails in Faerûn can be found in numerous other sources both prior to and following this book's publication. This could be interpreted as a case of in-universe misinformation, with Zakharans having little exposure to or knowledge of the many Faerûnian ships that do sport lateen sails. Ships that sported a lateen sail were referred to as "lateen-rigged".

Description
Unlike most sails in Faerûn, which took on a rectangular shape, lateens were triangular.Golden Voyages describes lateens or at least a variant of lateen as not being true triangles, but as having a corner cut off. This would instead refer to what is known as a  settee sail This shape allowed lateens to remain effective at sizes larger than rectangular sails and enabled ships to sail more windward.

Availability
In the city of Waterdeep, the Most Diligent League of Sail-makers and Cordwainers sold lateen sails of "standard" dimensions for around 500 to 700 gold pieces, though could lower those prices by thirty percent if several months' notice were given. Some Waterdhavians unaffiliated with the guild sold sails for as low as 200 gold pieces, though they carried no guarantees.

History
The existence of lateens on Toril dated as far back as the Netherese Empire, where they saw some use on small caravels used to sail the Narrow Sea.

Notable Users
Named Ships
 The Gleason, a galleass belonging to the Dhostars, was outfitted with lateen sails.
 Queen of the Night, a merchant galley belonging to the Zhentarim, had two lateen-rigged masts.
 Sea Sprite, a caravel captained by Deudermont, was outfitted with three lateen-rigged masts.
 Wail of the Banshee, a unique galleon style necromancer ship, had two lateen-rigged masts.
 Wave Wyvern, a galleass belonging to Grand Duke Farnig Seasilver, had three lateen-rigged masts.
Organizations
 The City Navy of Waterdeep had over twenty small galleys sporting lateen sails.
Regions, Nations, & Settlements
 Amnian caravels were typically outfitted with lateen sails.
 In the city of Marsember, both civilians and soldiers used canvas covers on their skiffs as makeshift lateen sails.
 The people of Lantan were widely known for their lateen-rigged ships.
Types of Ships
 Battle dolphins, a type of spelljammer, were outfitted with a lateen sail on their forward deck.
 Coastboats, a type of vessel used throughout Aglarond to sail along its coast and many rivers, were typically outfitted with lateen sails.
 Feluccas, a type of twin-masted vessel used by fisherfolk in Rashemen, was typically lateen-rigged.
 Folding boats commonly sported a lateen sail.
 Galleasses.
 Gigs, a type of sail boat found in Calimshan, were typically outfitted with lateen sails.
 In Zakhara, lateen sails were commonly used on dhows, such as barijahs, baghlas, and sambuks. Beyond dhows, qaribs were sometimes outfitted with lateens and achieved faster speeds with such rigging.

## Largo Crivens the Younger

Largo Crivens the Younger was one of the inhabitants of Reithwin, the town that once venerated Selûne in the Western Heartlands, sometime before the late 14 century DR.

History
Largo perished in a tragic event sometime before the fall of Reithwin, before the, and before it was consumed by the Shadow-cursed Lands. The event took the lives of Largo and almost every member of the Crivens clan, including the family dog, outlived only by Errick Crivens, who was, in some way, the cause of the tragedy. Largo Crivens the Younger was buried in Reithwin's graveyard behind the House of Healing in a cluster surrounded by the graves of his family. The tombstone read: "Here lies Largo Crivens the Younger, taken along with his family on the same grim night. May their deaths be avenged." The Crivens family's grave markers remained relatively undamaged and legible as of the .

## Lampris

Lampris was the unfortunate younger brother of Parzal the Outrageous, who lived prior to the destruction of Netheril in .

Relationships
Lampris's father was a ranger who had traveled from another world, and his mother was a spellcaster from the far east of Faerûn. He had an older brother, Parzal.

History
As an infant, his family home was attacked by a sohei from the Monastery of the West Wind. The sohei slew his parents, robbed and burned the home, and kidnapped Parzal and Lampris. The sohei eventually abandoned Parzal in the Dunes of Death, and sold Lampris into slavery. Although Parzal searched for his brother as an adult, he never found Lampris again.

## Larani

Larani was the proprietor of the Wayfork Inn in the Wayfork Village along the Trade Way in the late 14 century DR.

History
In the late 14 century DR, the Red Fang Marauders began raiding the environs of Baldur's Gate and attacked caravans on the Trade Way. One such raid was led by Harnak the Butcher. The goblins attacked Wayfork Village, forcing the survivors to flee and shelter in Larani's inn. Harnak and his men broke into the inn, but the survivors and Larani herself were saved by a group of six heroes who ended the hobgoblin's life and fought the raiders off. Larani was grateful and even collected some humble coin to reward the adventurers, but despite that, she still believed that adventurers were rough and dangerous kind.

## Lamona

Lamona was a Sharran Fidelian – a mage in the hidden Cloister of Sombre Embrace in the city of Baldur's Gate in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Lamona was a wood elven woman with a pale skin complexion, watery grey eyes, tattooed neck, and white hair streaked with pink. She wore Sharran Fidelian robes and hid her face behind a white porcelain mask adorned with Shar's holy symbol.

Abilities
Like her fellow Sharran Fidelians, Lamona wielded a quarterstaff in battle and was proficient with dark magics that created clouds of darkness. She herself was not hindered by such clouds as Sharran Fidelians were born into darkness – they could freely see in magical darkness and could never be blinded.

History
In the, during the Absolute crisis, Lamona served the Mother Superior Viconia DeVir in the underground Cloister of Sombre Embrace as one of her cultists underneath the House of Grief. Lamona, along with the entirety of the cloister may have confronted its rogue member named Shadowheart, were she to turn away from Shar. Lamona could have perished in the struggle. By other accounts, Fidelian and the rest of the cloister helped defend the city of Baldur's Gate from the army of the Absolute, were Shadowheart to remain loyal to her evil deity, or if Shadowheart was betrayed and surrendered to the Mother Superior for punishment.

## Lamantha's mortuary

Lamantha's mortuary was a business owned by the necromancer Lamantha that provided mortician and taxidermy services to the undead inhabitants of Evernight in the Shadowfell.

Location
It was located north of the river of lava that flowed through Evernight.

Services
As a mortician and taxidermist, much of Lamantha's work involved preparing bodies (or parts of bodies) as decorations, sporting equipment, tools, or trophies for her undead clientele. She was also involved in preparing living humanoids to be killed and eaten by the undead of Evernight in ritualistic "funerals" held in the Graveyard. This included sedating the victim with a powerful paralytic (derived from the humors of ghouls), then cleaning the victim, dressing them in fine clothes, and delivering them to the Temple of Filth.

Lamantha also provided "healing" to injured undead as well as cosmetic services to make them appear to be alive.

History
Owing to the fact that Evernight's undead did not harass Lamantha in recognition of her services, her mortuary was one of the only places in Evernight where living people might find shelter as of  (assuming Lamantha could tolerate them).

## Lamantha

Lamantha was a human necromancer who dwelt in the Shadowfell city of Evernight as of the late 15 century DR. She was the proprietor of a mortuary that provided a variety of macabre services.

Personality
She was presumed to be insane, and was willing to commit many heinous and horrific acts against living humanoids. She was impatient with other people, but not always hostile. On occasion, she was happy to encounter another living person if it meant that she could converse with them about interesting topics, notably current events in the Material Plane. She might even offer such people a safe place to stay, but she would lose interest in them within a couple of days.

Activities
Lamantha worked as a mortician and a taxidermist, and was also involved in preparing the victims of religious executions—known as "funerals"—carried out by the Temple of Filth. In addition to running her business—which provided services to the undead of Evernight that ranged from "healing" to cosmetics to sedation of living people—she devoted herself to researching and observing the undead inhabitants' growth and change.

Relationships
Lamantha was one of very few living people allowed to live in Evernight. The city's ruling ghouls left her alone in exchange for the services she provided at her mortuary.

History
As of, no one was sure of Lamantha's true purpose for staying in Evernight.

## Ladder

Ladders were handy (but bulky) tools that allowed people to climb up walls.

Description
A ladder was basically two parallel wooden poles connected by a series of evenly-spaced rungs. It was used by leaning it against a wall, and once in place it allowed the user to reach the higher vantage point. A ladder could also be set horizontally across a gap, creating a simple bridge, though they did not bear the weight as easily then.

Exceptionally large ladders used for scaling castle walls were known as siege ladders. Though a simple and direct solution, siege ladders were highly dependent on the defenders being taken by surprise or otherwise distracted, often in conjunction with other siege engines.

Rope ladders were flexible and able to be bundled up, while spider poles were a collection of metal rods that could be assembled into a climbing frame. The ladder of Elminster was a performer's piece that could stand up without support.

Availability

Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue offered a selection of ladders for the discerning climber which could support  vertically and  horizontally. They were made of duskwood and came in  (7 sp),  3 gp,  (5 gp), and  (10 gp) lengths. For an addition gold piece, the 10" and 20" ladders could be fitted with hooks that allowed them to be connected to each other or hung from windows and roofs.

Magic
If one was unable to procure a ladder, then the ladder, smoke ladder, or force ladder spells would allow an ambitious spellcaster to imitate their power.

## Lathe

A lathe was a mechanical device used by artisans to work various materials.

Usage
Wood, metal, and stone could be turned on a lathe using specialized blade bits to cut the material, creating regularly shaped staves, spindles, columns, rods, candlesticks, and other rounded components used for weapons, tools, furniture, and other items. A power source such as a water mill could be used to operate a lathe, sometimes with a gear to adjust the speed appropriately. Maillers used a lathe that didn't carve the material, but instead coiled wire into loops that were then cut and riveted, ultimately being made into chain mail.

Availability
Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue offered a 20 gp lathe that could hold a  by  piece of stone or wood to be worked. They also offered woodcutting bits (5 gp) and stonecutting bits (15 gp) alongside it.

## Last Light Inn

The Last Light Inn was a tavern located in Reithwin in the Western Heartlands in the late 14 and 15 centuries DR. It served as the secret base of the Selûnite resistance by the innkeeper Halfred to oppose Ketheric Thorm, until the general's fall. It remained abandoned for a century until Isobel Thorm used it to hide from Ketheric, later attracting the attention of the Harpers.

Geography

The tavern was located just outside the town, beside the River Chionthar. Two bridges across the water allowed access to the Last Light, though since the shadow curse, only the southeast bridge was usable. A small burial ground lay to the northeast, as well as a small house.

Structure

The inn was a two-story building; the ground floor was the taproom featuring the bar and a large number of tables curtained-off into booths by various tapestries, as well as a couple of rooms for guests. On the second floor, there extra rooms and a balcony overlooking the River Chionthar. The basement was smaller, and was mainly used for storage. It did, however, have a secret exit that led to a small enclave and house just outside. The basement was primarily used by Halfred to help organize the Selûnite resistance.

In addition, the Inn housed a dock from the Chionthar, allowing small boats to dock underneath. Outside of the main building itself, a small forge allowed those versed in blacksmithing to craft weapons and armor.

History
14 Century
Around 1373 DR, the then owner of the inn, Halfred, secretly met and organized with those loyal to Selûne to oppose Ketheric Thorm and his Dark Justiciars. On his death, Ketheric laid a shadow curse on the land, forcing Reithwin and the Last Light Inn to be abandoned for around a hundred years.

15 century
After being resurrected by Balthazar in the late 1400s DR, Ketheric's daughter Isobel, horrified with her father's change, fled to the abandoned Last Light Inn there. There, she cast a spell that enveloped the building and a small portion of the surrounding area in Selûne's grace, protecting it from the shadow-curse. This created a safe haven for those seeking shelter, for so long as Isobel herself remained alive.

In the, agents of the Harpers and Flaming Fist headed by Jaheira came to Reithwin to investigate Ketheric's own return from the dead. Jaheira and Isobel became acquainted and Isobel gifted the groups with a blessing of Selûne providing a limited protection from the shadow curse when outside the inn, as Isobel herself needed to stay at the Last Light to maintain concentration on the moon shield. Soon thereafter, a group of adventurers came to the location seeking protection from the shadow-curse, and met Jaheira and her Harpers, who directed them to Isobel.

## Lavernica

Lavernica was a naturalist and a groundskeeper of Bloomridge Park in the Lower City of Baldur's Gate in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Lavernica was described as a "lover of birds, bees, and everything in between." Judging by a historic log of the Emerald Enclave's activities found in the groundskeeper's home, Lavernica was possibly affiliated with the druidic organization.

History
In the, Lavernica was a busy groundskeeper who diligently cleaned fallen branches and blocked pathways off, polished stone benches, cleaned ponds, and unlocked the park gates every morning. The daily afternoon tasks included raking leaves, killing pests, repairing signage, and evening gate locking. Lavernica's journal had detailed notes on botany and plant care. It also noted that Lavernica witnessed some suspicious lurkers in the park and wanted to notify the Flaming Fist mercenaries. This was what ended the groundskeeper's life. Lavernica was targeted and surely murdered by the Bhaalist Murder Tribunal run by Orin the Red. The groundskeeper's home, just by the park's eastern gates, was bathed in violence; numerous pet giant spiders were killed inside, as well as duergar and drow, whose gore was turned into an unholy effigy dedicated to the Lord of Murder.

## Lasher

Lashers were martial combatants that devoted themselves to mastery over whips above all other weapons, learning to wield them as though they were extensions of their body.

Culture
The life of a lasher was typically taken up by barbarians, fighters, rangers, as well as some former druids and paladins. It was also seen as an attractive path by some bards, monks, and rogues.

Lashers delighted in showing off their skills, considering the use of whips to be as much of an art form as it was a martial art.

Abilities
Lashers were such masters in the art of whips that they could use them to grab or interact with far away objects or even disarm their foes. The most skilled of lashers had such a transcendent understanding of whips that they gained a supernatural power to rapidly whirl their weapon above their heads, incapacitating foes within a  radius.

Due to their proficiency with wielding whips, lashers often had skills in leatherworking and in the proper usage of ropes.

Notable Lashers
 Sardak, an expert half-elf lasher and bandit leader who wielded both a +3 keen sweeping mighty whip dagger and a masterwork whip.

## Larethian

Larethian was a religious tome dedicated to the elvish deity Corellon Larethian, penned by Palmer Junisugga sometime before the late 14 century DR.

Contents
The book claimed that the race of elves was created by Corellon's tears, mixed with tears of Sehanine Moonbow, the elvish deity known as the Lady of Dreams. The author found that fact curious, as it was the physiological reverse of a common creation myth. The elves were created from an expression of sorrow rather than being formed from the deity's hip bone or other bodily bits. Some sages theorized that this act of creation was countering Corellon's feelings of loss and betrayal at the time. Another opinion on the subject shared in the book came from the sage Aephir Loquellan, an elf who believed that the species being born from tears meant elves were "as sad as a species can be," due to elves enduring the burdens of living for numerous centuries.

History
In the, a copy of Larethian was available to guests of the Stormshore Tabernacle among many other religious books for perusal. It was also sold at The Bibliophile, a book shop in the Lower City of Baldur's Gate. Another copy was kept in the sinister House of Hope, the abode of a powerful devil Raphael in Avernus.

## Laeron

Laeron was a decrepit mummy who dwelled in an ancient Netherese tomb beneath the Stonelands.

Abilities
Laeron had a number of arcane spells at his disposal, including chain lightning, displacement, Otiluke's freezing sphere, protection from elements, mass, and wail of the banshee.

History
Laeron was interred in his tomb with a number of other undead and servitors, including wheeps, quells, skeletons, and tomb guardian constructs. Around the time of the Darkening in the late 15 century DR, the adventurers Jarus Brithandir, Kirina Aelorothi, Tristran of Daggerford, Popkin Shortshanks, Helayna, and Liantor Stoneborn discovered the tomb and cut their way through a few of the guardians before finding a passage hidden behind Laeron's throne. The adventurers passed deeper underground into natural caverns below the tomb, and by the time Laeron was up and about they were long gone. A second wave of invaders arrived to rescue the first group, and while Laeron and his minions put up a strong fight he was destroyed.

## Laerion'vyr

Mistress Laerion'vyr was a drow high priestess of Lolth who ruled a small drow settlement beneath the Stonelands in the late 15 century DR.

History
Sometime around the Darkening, Laerion'vyr controlled a sizable group of drow, including driders, lesser priestesses, a draegloth bodyguard, and a cadre of ritualists led by Vur'din that summoned a number of slaadi allies, including the powerful gray slaad Il'tharis the Terrible. The drow had a contentious relationship with a nearby group of subterranean lizardfolk, and their presence may have been discovered by adventurers that plumbed the adjacent Netherese tomb and confronting the red dragon Daeronnax.

## Laothan

Laothan was the northernmost nation of Malatra in Kara-Tur. It was home to the Seng people. Though it was a unified kingdom like the neighboring Kuong Kingdom, the ruling Thok house only directly ruled over the upper Phu River basin from their capital of Cheinang, with other princes and the lesser nobles who served them autonomously ruling over their ancestral territories.

Geography
The territory of Laothan swept in a curve along the northern coast of Malatra. It bordered the Purang and the Malatran Plateau to the south and west, the Kuong Kingdom to the south (along the Dwai River), the empire of T'u Lung to the northwest, and the Celestial and Eastern Seas to the north and east, respectively. The traditional southern border with Kuong was marked by the ruins of the fortress of Tua Koh, which had been fought over between the two nations many times.

Laothan was covered in a vast rainforest fed by monsoons, various river systems, and a humid tropical climate; this rainforest was of light density in the north and east and medium density in the Phu River basin south and west of Cheinang. The terrain of Laothan was hilly and dominated by steep ridges, and most agriculture was thus done at the bottoms of valleys or on hillside terraces.

Geographic Features
Rivers
Dwai River • Phu River

Language
The people of Laothan spoke the Laothan language. As they came into contact with neighboring civilizations, they borrowed from their languages words they did not yet have concepts for. Most of these loanwords originated from the T'u Lung language, but Laothan pronunciation was so different that they were unrecognizable to Shou ears.

People
The Seng people were of similar race to those of neighboring T'u Lung, having fine, straight black hair, epicanthic folds, and brown eyes. They had a joyful approach to life, celebrating many yearly celebrations and festivals, and they were known to be far more independently-minded and contentious than the Kuong to the south.

Government
Laothan was a monarchy ruled by the Thok clan, which ruled from Cheinang. However, the king's rule over most of the country was not direct; instead, a number of princes ruling over their ancestral lands answered to Cheinang, an arrangement originally forced upon Thok Hueng after his unification of Laothan. Relatives of the Thok clan received their power directly from the king and were the most loyal and influential members of the Laothan aristocracy. In the mid–14 century DR, Thok Lian reigned as the king of Laothan.

Minor nobles served under the many petty princes of Laothan, and it was from these princes that these nobles got their power and land grants, though this was typically just to confirm their traditional ownership of land and their authority. Outside of Cheinang, where the Thok clan had centralized their rule, these minor nobles and land-holding aristocrats formed a network that oversaw the domestic affairs of Laothan, which were carried out on a local basis.

Religion
Among the Seng of Laothan, the Path of Enlightenment, which they had adopted from the Shou (with whom they had the most contact out of all the peoples of Malatra) was the most popular religion. In particular, the dominant school was the Ku Nien school, which provided guidelines for personal behavior, social formalities, and obligations of duty in addition to religious ritual practices and chanting meditations and promised enlightenment for those who adhered to its structures and practices. The Ku Nien school was represented in Laothan by a system of interconnected temples and monasteries dedicated to the school's practices.

Economy
As with other lowland regions of Malatra, the economy of the Laothan lowlanders was based around the cultivation of rice and supplemental fruits and vegetables. In fact, the Seng had the most extensive system of rice-farming villages throughout Malatra, and their land was thus the most agriculturally well-developed. This sparked the ambitions of the Kuong Kingdom, whose lands were less agriculturally developed because of the dense jungle that the Kuong inhabited and the slash-and-burn agricultural techniques that many Kuong villages employed, in annexing Laothan.

In addition, the Seng were skilled in leatherworking and woodworking, though they traded few of the resulting products outside of Laothan. Silver was mined in Kwang Tre, and unlike the aforementioned leather and wood products, jewelry from southern Laothan was popular among not just the Seng but also the Kuong as well.

Relations
Owing to the geography of the region, the nations of Malatra were protected from most external threats but also constrained together. As a result, the Kuong, Seng, and Purang had long fought over each others' territory, with borders being pushed back and forth several times. Laothan most often butted heads with the Kuong Kingdom, which had sought to exploit its fertile lands since at least the 12 century DR; this rivalry was reflected in the views of one particularly prejudiced Kuong nun, who often derided the Seng and (among other things) referred to them and the Purang as "so-called civilizations." As of 1357 DR, the conflicts between the two kingdoms had been in a lull for 50 years, but as a result, the petty princes of Laothan began to squabble amongst themselves. This opened up an opportunity for the Kuong Kingdom, which had been building up its army in those years, to invade once more.

The other main neighbor with which Laothan interacted was T'u Lung. Though the two nations had close cultural ties, with the Seng adopting their northern neighbors' religion and many words from their language, the nature of Laothan's diplomatic relationship with T'u Lung in the mid–14 century DR is unclear, but in the year 594 in a local calendar, the fortress of Dien Nyu was constructed on the border of T'u Lung on Nuyen Sing's orders to deter raiders.The date is simply given as "in 594", but it is unknown what calendar this is in. No other dates are given in The Jungle Lands chapter in Kara-Tur: The Eastern Realms, it's unclear which other calendar should be presumed, and there no dateable connections to this event. (Given that the narrator is a Kuong nun, one possibility is that the calendar in question is the one used within the Kuong Kingdom, though even that assumption leaves plenty of ambiguities.)

Culture
Men wore simple loincloths or loose trousers and shirts while doing manual labor. In contrast, women wore high-necked, form-fitting dresses with long slits up the side. Those who had to do manual labor (including village women) combined a looser version of this dress with trousers underneath. Both sexes favored silks that were hand-painted and dyed in bright colors.

Seng cuisine was known to be hot and spicy.

The countryside of Laothan was dominated by villages of huts made of woven bamboo and palm leaf and built upon raised platforms propped up by stilts, a setup that offered protection from insects and wildlife and kept the floors of said huts dry during the rainy monsoon seasons. The village architecture of Laothan was similar to that of the Purang to the south, but their structures were more elaborate and sturdy, incorporating hardwoods from the jungles of Laothan. The dwellings of nobles often consisted of interconnected houses, some of which were as high as two stories tall. The fortresses and defensive structures of the Seng were built upon stone foundations, but the actual buildings were made of wood; the same could be said of the temples and shrines in Laothan.

Society
There was some yakuza activity in Laothan, though it was nowhere near as common as in the Kuong Kingdom. More commonly found in Laothan were ninja from T'u Lung who had settled in Sayan, offering their services to the highest bidders. The largest and most active of the ninja clans of Sayan were the Sung clan, who operated throughout Laothan and even conducted espionage missions in the Kuong Kingdom under the guise of merchants. Despite being based in Sayan, from where the prominent Nuyen house ruled, the Sung clan did not necessarily support the Nuyen princes, instead being primarily concerned with keeping travel routes open to facilitate their work.

Unlike in the Kuong Kingdom, where wu jen were severely persecuted and even publicly executed if caught, wu jen in Laothan were viewed with more reverence, being feared and respected by the Seng, and they could be found in some cities within Laothan.

In Laothan, blade dancers, shadow scouts, and shintao monks were fairly common.

History
Prior to the mid–12 century DR, the Seng were divided amongst many different tribal kingdoms that each ruled over a relatively small territory. The rapidly expanding Kuong Kingdom to the south coveted the fertile lands they inhabited, and it launched several invasions of these petty kingdoms. The Kuong even managed to capture the Seng fortress of Tua Koh, though it would pass back and forth between the Kuong and the Seng several times before the conclusion of the conflicts. In response, King Thok Hueng united the Seng under his banner in the mid–12 century DRThis is "almost 200 years" before a setting date of 1357 DR, so some time after 1157 DR. and successfully led their resistance against the invaders, though Tua Koh was destroyed during the final siege.

From around 1300 DR, the Kuong Kingdom apparently abandoned its ambitions in Laothan, leading to five decades of peace between the rival nations. In fact, the Kuong had spent those years consolidating their western holdings and building up their military. In that time, the princes of Laothan, taking their freedom from Kuong expansion for granted, grew resentful of the ruling Thok house, wishing for a return to earlier times in which they would have been independent kings in their own right.

Modern History
In the 1350s DR, Thok Lian found himself struggling to hold the throne of Laothan and keep his kingdom united in the face of the growing separatist movement. The most prominent of his opponents was Prince Nuyen Phi, the charismatic and arrogant patriarch of the Nuyen house, which ruled over northern Laothan, and commander of the largest military force in Laothan. Nuyen Phi, convinced that the Kuong Kingdom no longer posed a threat to Laothan and thus seeing no need to maintain the "convenient fiction" (as he saw it) that the Seng were united under Thok rule, had begun ignoring the edicts of Thok Lian, though he had yet to take any action against him. Of course, he was sorely mistaken: spurred on by the agricultural bounty of Laothan and the urging of Lady Natiya, Priest-King Vishnan VII had begun plotting a new offensive against Laothan.

In 1356 DR, a Kuong temple sohei attempted to assassinate the great Seng wu jen Hueng, master of a school in Deikhou that trained wu jen in the art of magic. The attempt failed, and the sohei died as a result.

The following year, the Kuong army began its attack against Laothan, moving covertly through the hills of the Purang and entering Laothan in secret. Careful to avoid conflict with the Purang tribesmen, the Kuong offered gifts to the Blood Tree and Twisted Palm tribes, whose territory they passed through, to placate them. The tribe leaders were split amongst themselves as to whether or not they should accept the bribes; one faction advocated for accepting them and leaving the Kuong alone, while the other argued that such an action would be implicitly taking their side against Laothan and quietly prepared to attack the Kuong armies. Meanwhile, the Kuong troops assembled at a secret camp between Tua Koh and Kwang Tre; from there, they planned to take Kwang Tre and then embark down the Phu River to launch a surprise attack on the Seng capital of Cheinang, striking a decisive blow.

The attack on Kwang Tre was expected to be an easy victory for the Kuong, as they believed that its defenders would pose little to no meaningful resistance because of their laxness. However, the power play of High Priest Malvaya, who (among other things) concealed predictions by Kuong diviners that the invasion would end in disaster, posed a major obstacle for the Kuong.

Notable Locations
Cities
 Cheinang • Sayan
Towns
 Deikhou • Kwang Tre • Mhou • Nalamh • Nindamh • Pursai • Tran • Thok Treng
Villages
 Bang Nu • Binkohn • Buyan • Khon Tre • Kompoa • Min Loh • Nyu Thok • Thiam
Fortresses
 Dien Nyu • Tua Koh

Appendix
Behind the Scenes
Laothan appears to be inspired by the medieval kingdom of Dai Viet (later Vietnam), whose culture and language were heavily influenced by that of China to the north, though its name instead suggests Lan Xang (later Laos). One notable parallel is in the description of Laothan's traditional attire, which may have been inspired by ao dai.

Its government, which appears to follow the mandala model that dominated much of pre-colonial Southeast Asia, may be analogous to that of the Ly dynasty, whose direct control was limited to the land surrounding its capital while other areas were controlled by autonomous lords who were allied to the royal clan (though the following Tran dynasty centralized control over its territory).

## Lathander's Dew

Lathander's Dew was a soft drink that came in several flavors, including the original, Code Reth, Moonshae Blue, and Icewind Freeze.

Description
Lathander's Dew was advertised as dew from the Sword Mountains. In reality, it was not dew, but it was effervescent water sourced from the Sword Mountains, dyed a greenish color, and flavored.

Code Reth was a red concoction flavored with cherry, likely referencing deadly gladiatorial battles in Reth of the Vilhon Reach. The red dye used in the drink was extracted from tiny scalebugs.

Moonshae Blue was flavored with a citrus fruit native to the Moonshae Isles but dyed blue to evoke the color of the Sea of Moonshae.

Icewind Freeze was dyed white – the color of Icewind Dale snow. Its flavor was a mystery, best served on ice.

## Law mantle

The Law mantle was a psionic mantle that granted ardents and divine minds an array of psionics powers and abilities derived from the universal concept of law.

Deities

Abilities
Manifesters of this mantle gained the unique psionic ability to expend their psionic focus to cause greater harm to any creature that wasn't lawfully-aligned with their next attack using a melee weapon, ranged weapon, or spell. Additionally, the psychic aura exuded by divine minds of this mantle granted the manifester and their nearby allies greater resistance towards attacks and effects by nonlawful creatures.

Powers
Novice manifesters were able to improve their martial abilities with grip of iron and hammer, as well as secure any container or gateway with psionic lock. Manifesters of some experience could alter gravity in an area with heavy earth and make themselves impossible to budge with immovability. As a manifester progressed further they learned how to steel their minds against the effects of others with steadfast perception and tower of iron will. Masters of this mantle obtained the powers of psionic iron body and timeless body.

## Ladystone

The Ladystone was a rock formation sacred to Eilistraee in the Ardeep Forest. It lay in the center of the Dancing Dell.

Description
The Ladystone was a finger or needle of rock standing in the center of the Dancing Dell. It was about breast-high to Qilué Veladorn, and had sides rough enough to draw blood, though long millenia of worship had ground out some of the roughness.

Powers
The Ladystone was part of the defenses of the Dancing Dell. It could strike as commanded by the priestesses. Eilistraeean priestesses could meditate upon it to commune with the Dancing Lady.

History
The origin of the Ladystone was unknown, but it was believed to date back to the earliest settlements of Ardeep. It was sacred to worshippers of Eilistraee, and it was said to have been touched by the goddess herself.

The wizard Tanarthil of the Towers made his way into the Dancing Dell at some point during a rite; after thwarting his spells, Qilué Veladorn urged her fellow priestesses not to chase after the man upon his departure. They were reticent to heed him, listening only after the Ladystone displayed the sign of Mystra.

## Laebos

Laebos was the elder rune of fire.

Usage
The Laebos rune was marked to indicate a place of fire or where fire was commonly employed, from the use of firewood and kindling, to the occurrence of lava and molten rock, to the presence of fire-breathing or fire-using creatures. It warned a traveler to take proper precautions against flame and heat. An enchanted weapon with powers of light, fire, and warmth might also be marked with Laebos.

Laebos referred to natural fire or extraplanar fire, not to heavenly fire, which was indicated by the Ingolth rune.

Halaster Blackcloak, the Mad Mage of Undermountain, placed elder runes imbued with magical power around the dungeon as traps and tricks for the unwary. The Laebos rune would either blast explorers with fire or make their weapons flaming.

## Largrond

Largrond of the Lash was a Red Wizard of Thay in the mid-14 century DR.

Personality
Though still overconfident and reckless, he was willing to take a few moments to discuss strategy before engaging in tough fights.

Abilities
He was known to be able to cast ironguard and antimagic field. Either his skills or those of Largrond were considered to outstrip Azmyrandyr's.

Possessions
He wore numerous rings.

Relationships
At the time of his death, he was a guest in a Sharran temple alongside Ylondan. He was a Red Wizard.

History
In, the Simbul assaulted a temple of Shar somewhere in Thay where the Red Wizard Azmyrandyr was preparing for an intrigue. Her goal was a revenge attack after Azmyrandyr participated in an attack on Silverymoon. After slaying Azmyrandyr and his apprentices, she continued to slay around the temple; the temple master, Maeldur, called on one of his underlings to rouse two visitors, those being Ylondan and Largrond.

The two quickly joined the fight after casting ironguard, and briefly discussed their strategy, right in front of her. Largrond cast an antimagic field, while Ylondan cast a spell to shatter the ceiling on top of her. They were aiming at a projected image of hers instead of the real her, who cast a spell to portal the rubble on top of them, killing them instantly.

## LaFontaine

LaFontaine was a pious adventuring human, active in the late 14 century DR.

LaFontaine was a tall and muscular human man with a light skin tone and flowing long hair. His armor of choice was scale armor.

History
In Kythorn of the, Waterdeep – the City of Splendors, became once again menaced by the drow from the mega-dungeon of Undermountain. The Masked Lords determined that the danger was coming from underneath the Yawning Portal, and Durnan, the proprietor of the famed establishment, put out a call for adventurers willing to traverse the dungeon's depths and route the evil within.

LaFontaine was among the heroes who answered the call, eager to spread the faith of his goddess and to practice his healing arts. LaFontaine was present at the Yawning Portal's common room, when a squadron of drow assassins sent by "queen" Valsharess attacked. The dark elves slaughtered many of the gathered adventurers. Survivors chased the assassins down into the Undermountain, while those killed were resurrected by the city's clergy, including LaFontaine. LaFontaine thought his skills would be of more use helping hurt and the dead and refused to venture into the Undermountain, leaving the quest in the hands of the hero who would be known as the Slayer of Mephistopheles.

Two repeat customers of his' were the thieves Farl and Eladar the Dark; the latter was secretly Prince Elminster Aumar of Athalantar.

History
Lansibilin sold pies to Farl and Eladar on the morning after they put together the Velvet Hands thieves' guild. Elminster thought there was a little less rat than usual on his'.

## Large spider

Large spiders were spiders considerable larger than their small normal cousins, yet mostly acted like them.

Description
Large spiders, despite their name were, around  in diameter.

Behavior
They would scuttle about on walls, floors, and ceilings, weaving their webs and hunting for prey. They would build strong sticky webs, then lurk nearby waiting for prey to become entangled. Only the strongest of persons would be unaffected, as most would at least have some difficulty removing themselves from the webs, allowing the large spiders to strike.

Combat
Large spiders would bite and inject their prey with a weak, but deadly, poison.

Ecology
Habitat
Large spiders were found in the starwood parts of the great Cormanthor forest, although they would sometimes be encountered in the midwood.

Relationship
Both Lolth and Zanassu were capable of calling upon large spiders to serve and fight for them at a moment's notice. They often served as familiars to magic-users such as necromancers.

Summoning
A large spider could be summoned by the summon monster II spell, while several could be called up by the summon spider spell, depending on the caster's ability.

History
Sometime around  a couple of large spiders were encountered in Ravens Bluff by a group of adventurers looking for a wererat.

Subspecies
Flying spiders were a rare subspecies of the large spider.

## Lakh varnish

Lakh varnish was a variety of varnish used in a variety of crafting methods for creatiing weapons, armor, and treating dinosaur leather.

Description
Lakh varnish was a powerful liquid created from terebinth and lakh resin. It was primarily used to finish wood and create lacquer.

When mixed with Tears of Ubtao, lakh varnish formed a magical substance called living varnish, used to create masterwork items in Chult. Other uses for lakh varnish were hardening and treating bronzewood, treating spruce wood for the construction of bucklers, strenghtening wooden barricades, crafting tlahuitolli, feathered teotlanextli, feathered ilhuilli, obsidian miztonhiyo, wootz scythes, and lacquering dinosaur leather.

## Lathlaeril

Laranlor Lathlaeril was the last ruler of Ardeep in the early 7 century DR. He was one of the three kings of Phalorm until his death.

History
Lathlaeril became the Laranlor of Ardeep in the, after the previous Laranlor, Ellatharion, vanished without a trace into the High Forest with his army as they routed a retreating force of orcs after the siege Secomber.

Lathlaeril himself died in the Battle of Firetears in the, which was fought against the Everhorde south of Triboar.

Legacy
After Lathlaeril's death, Ardeep remained an elven kingdom in name only; much of the elven population decided to depart for Evermeet by the .

## Layer 99

Layer 99 was the unnamed 99 layer of the Abyss.

Description
It consisted of seven sub-realms, each with completely different characteristics. Each sub-realm was adjacent to two, three. or four other realms. However since physical laws were different here, realms weren't always mutually adjacent, and instead obeyed their own capricious nature.

Visitors to Layer 99 could get visual clues about which realms were adjacent by observing the horizon: about one-third of it displayed the color associated with a neighboring realm. Traveling to another sub-realm could be accomplished by using special inter-dimensional devices or simply by walking, and sometimes even staring or concentrating long enough, toward the chosen horizon color.

Gray Realm
This realm resembled a desert of black sand and undulating hills, stretching endlessly in every direction. The landscape was utterly barren, and the sky above was choked with dense gray clouds. Roughly one-third of the horizon glowed with a yellow tint, another shimmered with a swirling mass of colors, and the final third was lit by silent lightning bolts tearing through the blackened sky. Blinding dust storms swept across the terrain with alarming frequency.

The only known inhabitants of this realm were the dretchs.

Lightning Realm
True to its name, lightning crackled across the blackened sky, and rain fell frequently upon the realm. The stark white terrain was punctuated by towering stone pillars, each rising miles into the air. These slender columns, no more than  thick, clustered in small groups every  or so. This realm often served as a battleground for the warring legions of Orcus and Demogorgon.

One-third of the horizon swirled with shifting colors, another was veiled in dense fog, and the final third glowed with a soft silver light—a clear indication of proximity to the Silver Realm.

Swirling Realm
This realm was defined by a sky of swirling colors and a churning ocean below—a grotesque parody of natural beauty. From a glistening hole suspended in mid-air, more than a mile (1.6 kilometers) above the surface, a roaring multicolored waterfall poured down, stretching at least  wide. It originated from a conduit leading to the Abyssal layer ruled by the loathsome demon lord Juiblex.

The waterfall and the ocean were composed not of water, but of oozes and jellies—dripping rot, fungoid secretions, and other vile matter seeping from Juiblex's domain. Despite the reversed gravity field of the realm, the ocean remained as buoyant as any ordinary sea, its surface littered with massive floating chunks of fungus. The only known inhabitants of this realm were vrocks, circling above or lurking within the gelatinous tides.

Half of the horizon crackled with streaks of lightning, while the other half shimmered with a soft silver glow—a clear sign of adjacency to the Silver Realm.

Yellow Realm
This sub-realm was a lush tropical forest, reminiscent of prehistoric jungles. Yet its scale defied comprehension: blades of grass towered  high, and palm leaves stretched as large as houses. The bright yellow sky was barely visible through the canopy, as the trees themselves soared more than a mile into the air.

One-third of the horizon shimmered with swirling colors, another crackled with flashes of lightning, and the final third was cloaked in a dull gray haze.

Known inhabitants of this realm included vrocks, achaierai, and thunder beasts.

Fog Realm
A thick gray fog prevailed in this realm, heavy with the stench of decay and rot, so dense that visibility was reduced to just . The ground beneath was slick and adhesive, reminiscent of walking on jellyfish. Bubbling and popping sounds constantly echoed around any interloper, while in the distance, muffled cries—eerily like those of infants—carried through the gloom. From far above drifted the screeching and cackling of bird-like creatures.

This realm served as an incubation zone for demon spawn. Shallow, swampy pits stretched for miles across its surface, each one brimming with writhing demon embryos. Vrocks frequently flew overhead, occasionally swooping down to snatch embryonic morsels from the muck.

The demon embryos were in various stages of development—from bloated embryonic sacs containing half-formed entities, to fully grown juvenile spawn already capable of movement and behaving aggressively.

A fourth of the horizon was saturated with swirling colors, another portion shimmered with stars against a backdrop of black sky, a third was tinged with a pale yellow hue, and the last radiated a soft silver glow.

Silver Realm
The landscape of this sub-realm consisted of flat, smooth sheets of silver ice, stretching for miles in every direction. It was blindingly bright and bitterly cold, especially for human-like creatures. The terrain was utterly featureless, devoid of sound or movement. No fires could be kindled here, and heat- or fire-based spells failed to function entirely.

The physical laws of the realm absorbed sound waves instantly, rendering speech useless. Those who entered had to rely on alternative means of communication.

Roughly one-third of the horizon was tinged with yellow light, while another third was cloaked in darkness and streaked with jagged lightning.

Star Realm
A desert of black sand stretched across this sub-realm, pocked with vast, shallow craters—each easily a hundred yards (90 meters) wide. Rivers of molten lava snaked between the pits, while steam and acrid smoke billowed from fissures in the scorched earth. Thousands of tormented souls writhed within the craters. Some staggered blindly between them, only to tumble into the lava or collapse into the pits. Emaciated and encrusted with filth, their faces were contorted in perpetual agony. Moans and screams echoed without end.

A number of lava mephits flitted above the craters, their glowing red bodies dripping molten rock. They reveled in the suffering, gleefully tormenting their victims. Overhead, several chasmes patrolled the skies, swooping down to attack any creature that aroused their suspicion.

The black sky shimmered with bright, pulsating stars—some of which vanished abruptly, only to reappear elsewhere. At intervals, gaping voids tore open in mid-air, disgorging fresh victims who plummeted, shrieking, into the craters below. Moments later, the rifts sealed and disappeared.

The chasmes here were demon slaves of Kali, tasked with retrieving fresh sacrifices for the dreaded Layer 500(her domain). The holes in the sky served as conduits, opening and closing to allow the demons passage. They also admitted cultists from other planes, who dropped screaming into the infernal landscape. The blinking stars were distant conduits, while a glowing red aperture marked a permanent gateway to Layer 500.

Should any interlopers enter this realm, they might remain unnoticed for a time—lava mephits and chasmes were vastly outnumbered, with hundreds of suffering souls for every demon. Though the victims could not speak, they would grope and clutch at outsiders, desperate for contact. While they posed no physical threat, their grasping limbs could hinder movement and delay escape.

This forsaken sub-realm of Layer 99 was the only known passage into the terrifying dominion of Kali.

One spring in the mid-to-late 14 century DR, the inhabitants of the Latimer farm took all their prized possessions and the best blades from their tools, including axes and scythes, fleeing to the nearby caves in response to Baron Vilhelm von Stag Falls turning into an undead.

## Land of the Beastly Brigands

The Land of the Beastly Brigands was a tiny nation of the Whamite Isles in the Sea of Fallen Stars. It participated in a series of wild conflicts against the other nations and pirates of the Whamite Isles at some point prior to the mass death of the population in the .

The Land of the Beastly Brigands was led by Badmuddin the Bald and Blikwik the Beast. As well as an abundance of thugs, the Beastly Brigands included the Bloody Buggers and the Beastlboats fleet.

The Land of the Beastly Brigands shared a land border with Agenais, which was marked by a mountain range. A part of its coastline looked out onto the Swil Passage, not too far from the smaller nation of Swil. One of the most notable landmarks of the land was the Black Cave.

## Lake Azul

Lake Azul, also known as Lake Calor was one of the four lakes surrounding Nexal in Maztica. It was dedicated to the Maztican god Azul.

Description
Lake Azul  and was the deepest and coldest of the four lakes surrounding Nexal. It lay on the north side of the city in the map in Viperhand, Douglas Niles has Lake Azul on the north side of the city and Lake Zaltec is on the east side (opposite from Lake Qotal), Poshtli also confirms this in chapter 2 of the same book, but in Ironhelm Lake Azul is referenced around page 65 as being on the east side and Lake Zaltec is on the north side. In even further confusion in chapter 20 of Viperhand, when the remnant of Cordell's legion are retreating on the causeway, it says that the causeway was between Lake Qotal and Lake Zaltec, but Lake Qotal was on their right and Lake Zaltec was on their left., and was the furthest lake from Mount Zatal to the south of the city. The causeway of the Kultaka road on its southeast side separated it from Lake Zaltec and the causeway of the West Road on its southwest side separated it from Lake Qotal.

History
As a result of it being the furthest from Mount Zatal, it was also the safest place to flee during the events of the Night of Wailing when Mount Zatal exploded and destroyed most of Nexal in 1361 DR. The horse Storm fled across this lake to escape Nexal during the catastrophe.

## Lake Zaltec

Lake Zaltec was one of the four lakes surrounding Nexal in Maztica. It was dedicated to the Maztican god Zaltec.

Description
Lake Zaltec was the largest of the four lakes surrounding Nexal, which was interpreted by Poshtli, prince of Nexal, to be because Zaltec was the god of war and war was the highest purpose, with no nation being better at war than Nexal. It lay on the east side of the city in the map in Viperhand, Douglas Niles has Lake Azul on the north side of the city and Lake Zaltec is on the east side (opposite from Lake Qotal), Poshtli also confirms this in chapter 2 of the same book, but in Ironhelm Lake Calor is referenced around page 65 as being on the east side and Lake Zaltec is on the north side. In even further confusion in chapter 20 of Viperhand, when the remnant of Cordell's legion are retreating on the causeway, it says that the causeway was between Lake Qotal and Lake Zaltec, but Lake Qotal was on their right and Lake Zaltec was on their left.. The causeway of the Kultaka road on its northwest side separated it from Lake Azul and the causeway of the road to Palul on its south side separated it from Lake Tezca.

History
Erixitl, chosen of Qotal, and her husband Halloran went across this lake by boat to re-enter the city of Nexal after it had been surrendered to the Golden Legion in 1361 DR. During the Night of Wailing, when Nexal was destroyed, a small surviving remnant of the legion attempted an escape on a causeway but masses of Maztican warriors in boats on Lake Zaltec  Again see note above: Niles says that they were on a causeway between Lake Qotal and Lake Zaltec, but this contradicts the geography in other places in the same series. According to Niles, the warriors were on boats who pulled Domincus were on the 'dark lake' (and in chapter 13 Lake Zaltec is referred to as having dark water).  pulled out the boards that connected the causeway in order to prevent their escape. Bishou Domincus, the Legion's cleric of Helm, was grabbed by a hook onto one of these boats and later sacrificed to Zaltec.

Following the destruction of Nexal, Zaltec himself took physical form in the city and walked across the lake to leave the city.

## Lake Tezca

Lake Tezca was one of the four lakes surrounding Nexal in Maztica. It was dedicated to the Maztican god Tezca.

Description
Lake Tezca was on the south side of Nexal, between the city and Mount Zatal, the volcano that loomed over Nexal. To its northeast it was separated from Lake Zaltec by the road to Palul and on its northwest it was separated from Lake Qotal by the desert road. Since it was on the south side, facing the sun and leading to the desert, it was named after Tezca, the Maztican sun god.

Following the destruction of Nexal, Zaltec himself took physical form in the city and walked across the lake to head south to Tewahca.

## Lake Qotal

Lake Qotal was one of the four lakes surrounding Nexal in Maztica. It was dedicated to the Maztican god Qotal.

Description
Lake Qotal was on the west side of Nexal. It was separated from Lake Tezca on the southeast by the desert road and it was separated from Lake Azul to its northwest by the west road. It was brackish saltwater and brown in colour. It was relatively shallow with tufts of grass and reeds extended far into the lake from the shore. It was small and stagnant, named after the god Qotal who had abandoned Maztica to the younger gods.

History

A portent appeared in Nexal sometime shortly before the Golden Legion arrived in Maztica when the all of the lakes around Nexal became turbulent, with the sole exception of Lake Qotal. Naltecona, the ruler of Nexal, thought that this was perhaps a sign of the return of Qotal to the world.

During the Night of Wailing, a few survivors from the Golden Legion managed to escape and reached the shore of Lake Qotal, which became very turbulent during Mount Zatal's eruption.

## Langmuir

Langmuir  was a small port village located on the isle of Moray within the Moonshae Isles.

Geography

The village was located on the eastern shore of Last Head at the very south of Moray. It was the nearest settlement to an important lighthouse located on the southern tip of the island, which was visible from the village only on clear days. This location meant that cloudy weather, fog, and light rain were common.

Government
Langmuir was ruled by a king and a council composed of the village's princes, merchants, mine owners, and minor nobles. The people generally trusted their rulers to make wise decisions. The council was known to engage in animated debates, sometimes around the fireplace in the common room of the village inn.

Defenses
The village was heavily fortified as of the late 15 century DR to defend against the Black Blood tribe. It had sturdy walls, but not enough soldiers to defend against a major, coordinated assault. Strangers arriving at the gates were not allowed inside until they proved that they were not lycanthropes by cutting themselves with a steel dagger. Lycanthropes were not allowed inside without support from a trusted member of the community, and likewise they refused entry to other shapechangers, any creature known to ally with the Black Blood (including goblins, orcs, half-orcs, and shifters), and any individuals who appeared to be part beast (such as gnolls or minotaurs).

Trade
Ore mined from the nearby Orcskull Mountains was brought into Langmuir where the valuable raw metals were extracted and then shipped to places like Alaron and Norland. The extraction and refinement of this metal ore was highly lucrative, making it worth the risks of mining and traveling in the dangerous mountains. Alchemical explosives were occasionally used in the mines, and the import and transportation of these substances were carefully controlled.

Langmuir was isolated and relied on trade. As of the late 15 century DR, the area around Langmuir was too dangerous to support largescale farming, and thus the people relied on food imported by ship to survive.

History
During the 15 century DR, lycanthropes of the Black Blood tribe frequently attacked Langmuir.

In the,The events of Darkness Falls over Moray are stated to take place one year after the events of Black Blood, which is presumed to take place in 1479 DR. a Black Blood werewolf known as Bloodeye began escalating attacks on the area and on mining caravans. A couple months later, a war hero from Alaron named Daffyd Mac Lyrr arrived in town to help coordinate efforts to drive him out, but less than two tendays later, Bloodeye captured and snuffed out the lighthouse near the village. The council sent a half-dozen of the best members of their militia to retake the lighthouse, but none returned. This led to a split in the council over whether to send their remaining militia on a second mission or to keep them in town in case of an attack.

Over the next three days, a longship called The Lightning wrecked just north of the village without the lighthouse to guide it and panic was spreading within Langmuir. Tensions rose when it was confirmed that a war band of the Black Blood tribe was amassing nearby, prompting the council to decide that the militia had to defend the village and adventurers needed to be found to strike at the Black Blood, for whom they turned to the crew of The Lightning led by Ulrik Ragnarsson. No agreement could be reached about whether to reclaim the lighthouse or attack the war band, and the decision was deferred to the adventurers. In the meantime, the Black Blood war band had been acquiring alchemical explosives from a spy within Langmuir, and were readying handcarts filled with such explosives to breach Langmuir's walls.

Inhabitants
By the late 15 century DR, most inhabitants were young people from abroad who came to work in Langmuir's lucrative but dangerous mining industry, whether as miners, ore refiners, caravanners, or guards. The people of Langmuir felt their very existence hinged on a losing battle against the lycanthropes of Moray, and because of the constant threat posed by these monsters, the townsfolk were highly suspicious of any "monstrous" individuals. Even if such individuals gained access to the village, they were mistrusted, feared, and were liable to be thrown in jail for the smallest infraction.

Notable Inhabitants
As of the late 15 century DR, notable inhabitants included:
 King Donald Mac Innis, who deferred rulership to his council.
 Jinkerton, a logistics clerk who secretly worked with the Black Blood tribe.
 Keeron Mac Innis, the son of King Donald Mac Innis.
 Maldwyn, head of a shipping consortium.
